=== FILE: Knee Reconditioning For a Weightlifter with Knee Pain [NCfH1q9ojWQ].txt ===

 what's up guys, Quinn Heineck here, just a little clinical case, little clinical athlete, anecdote, for you guys, I'm currently working with a young weightlifter and his coach for reconditioning for some tendon-related knee pain that he's experiencing during snatches, clean jerks, back squats, etc. That's a great situation as far as a kind of a team effort, I've got a good relationship with the coach, we're in communication, the athlete's on board, it's just a really good situation, the only bad part is that he's experiencing that tendon-related knee pain which can be really frustrating for a young explosive athlete who just wants to enjoy training but also express his physical attributes and he can't do it, it's frustrating. So we're working on that reconditioning plan but one of the things that we're incorporating, especially in the beginning, I've been working with him and his coach for several weeks now, is a no-feet snatch, no-feet snatches are common place in weightlifting training, a lot of times for technique where we're looking to just kind of smooth out somebody's footwork if they've got kind of crazy legs or they donky kick or they jump back or jump forward, so a no-feet snatch is kind of a conceptual constraint that you put on the lift to try to smooth out the footwork and it can help smooth out the pull and it can do a lot of good things for technique but in this case, this athlete was sensitive to the landing or the kind of the reabsorption phase when the feet leave and then hit the platform aggressively, that was triggering the knee pain. So in this case, the no-feet snatches worked as kind of a buffer for that so he's still able to get the lift in but it's a little bit less dynamic, less impactful, less triggering of the tendon pain and in the beginning we're more closed with that prescription, a little bit more stringent with it as the symptoms were more reactive and I want to get to know the athlete and see how he's responding to the movements and so we're more strict on the no-feet aspect of it but as we go, what we actually want is for organic movement strategies to emerge that are beneficial for performance, yes, but also start nudging their way towards, you know, the competition style of lifting. So what happened was and in the videos that you'll see if you swipe over is that he ended up hitting a double, a note, the program said no-feet snatches for doubles and he ended up working out to a weight that was five kilos heavier than his all-time double and was only eight kilos below his all-time best snatch and you'll notice that his feet do move a little bit so if we had to tick the box off, you know, technically are these no-feet snatches, no, but what happened was his feet moved just enough because he's kind of conditioned himself to learn how to put force into the platform and to, you know, rearrange his feet into his receiving stance without so much impact but now he's starting to do that and it's starting to boost performance at the same time so he's, he's, a strategy emerged organically. Well, we had those constraints in place and he's, he's trained this way now over time and his body's kind of learned a new strategy so in this case he's able to work up to a PR snatch double across the board, no-feet-feet regardless and also in a, in a more comfortable position. Now, does that mean that over time, hopefully as we keep reconditioning the, the knees, we get stronger, more, more tolerance, more capacity that his technique won't gravitate back towards more foot movement and more of a deliberate impact in the ground? Yeah, it certainly might and, and fine, you know, we want whatever strategy is going to be best for performance long-term but in the short term it was just cool to see that that constraint that we put in place, we want it to kind of organically emerge into something else, you know, it starts to resemble that more competition form over time and that's what happened. I thought it was just a cool little case for you so you can swipe over and actually look at the lifts and let me know if you have any questions in the comments.

Yeah, well, to just very quickly answer the question why it was thought of as a major component, I don't know because as I lay out in the paper, the evidence was never really all that strong. So where I start in the paper is with Curtains, I think it's 1941 paper published in Research Quarterly. So Research Quarterly is a very important journal in the history of exercise science and physical education and even physical therapy. And this is where a lot of papers that have influenced exercise science sort of started from. For medicine and science and sports and exercise before the ACSM existed and so on. And Curtains paper, what he was looking at was it was sort of a review mixed in with some original data that he collected on swimmers and then some control participants. And I think what he demonstrated there was that the swimmers had higher levels of flexibility than the controls, which is not unusual. That's fairly normal findings from what I see. But what's so interesting about that paper, and I pointed that out in my paper, is that when he went to correlate swim performance with the flexibility, not only did it not correlate, he didn't even bother to report the correlations because he basically said, oh, there's no significant correlation. Yes, things just somehow evolved over time and people still just couldn't, I sort of guess, escape the fact that maybe it's not all that important, especially, and this is really important with my paper is the broader perspective and context is in relation to all the other components of fitness. So that paper by Curtain is the main one that got it going, really, at least as far as my historical understanding. And then over the time, the 50s and the 60s, you continue to have similar sorts of papers like cross-technal studies looking at sex differences in flexibility, maybe age differences and so on and so forth. You just find some of these commentary sort of style pieces that just sort of say how important it is, but yet they'd actually, if you look at the pieces closely, there's not strong evidence there. There was one interesting paper, which one was that? Holland in 1968 was one person who finally said something, oh, actually, the correlation probably is not as strong as we believe. That was a really good review paper that I found, but it didn't stop the momentum. So I think in 1980, that's when school fitness, well, I got to take a step back. So in the 1950s is when we got in the US, the president's group on physical fitness, Dwight Eisenhower was the president at the time. And the whole reason for that, by the way, which is another interesting historical fact, which is that the whole reason in the US that we have this president's count on physical fitness and we do school-based fitness testing is that in the 50s, there was a study published in the journal Research Quarterly that showed that American school students had lower levels of physical fitness than European students. And that included a test of flexibility among many others. So it just goes to show you how important and influential certain research papers can be. It was the cause of what we do now. I'm American, so I went through all those school-based fitness tests. And that paper is the reason why. So in 1980s, it started to pick up, the student reach was included in the manual for physical fitness testing, and it's been a part of it ever since. And I believe it still is. So just to anchor the conversation a little bit, we define and redefine flexibility in the paper with a couple of references as referring to the intrinsic properties of body tissues that determine maximal joint range of motion without causing injury. So with that, it can be a pretty nonspecific term, flexibility. So there's probably a lot of things that encompass the range of motion that is expressed. And you dig into that a little bit and you differentiate between dynamic flexibility, which is kind of our volitional motor drive of doing some type of movement pattern, or dictated by the muscle tendon stiffness and some of the properties therein. But at the paper, we're dealing more here with static flexibility. What we would think of as traditionally where we're not volitionally contracting the muscle and we're just looking to go to whatever that end range is. And you make an interesting point that that is subjective. Flexibility is, static flexibility is a subjective measure in a sense because you're really going to the tolerance of the person rather than to their true physiological end range. As in, if we could do like a thought experiment and you stretch to your perceived end range and then I snapped a finger and all of a sudden you were asleep or under anesthesia, you would hypothetically be able to go further. When you use that hip strategy, what ends up happening is that you end up with a very, very stiff knee. You can only absorb so much force at the hip by going through that strategy, and this is where I'll say, I'm perfectly fine with somebody using a hip strategy as long as they're also flexing their knee. If you overload the hip in the sagittal plane by flexing the hip, then you'll have to find another plane of movement that typically is going to be internal rotation. And this is your classic valgus. You go into internal rotation. If you leave the knee close to straight and you go into internal rotation, you're essentially putting all of the load right into the ACL, and this is how you end up with an injury. And so when something, when you're looking at efficacy, what you're asking is in a perfect ideal situation, what happens? So and if it is, if it's successful there, then you can say that it is efficacious. Now, so you could say like a blood pressure medication, you take the blood pressure medication, it actually lowers blood pressure. It is efficacious. If I control for everything, does it actually do the thing I said it did that it's going to do? Yes, it does. Now, when you go to effectiveness, what you're asking is now that I take this thing that is shown to be efficacious, and I'm going to put it in the real world, does it actually succeed in the real world? So again, your blood pressure medication lowers blood pressure. But the goal of lowering somebody's blood pressure is not just arbitrarily to lower blood pressure. It's to prevent what they call all cause mortality. You're trying to keep somebody from dying. And so you need to test that when you apply it in the real world, does it actually reduce, mortality? And in the case of like a blood pressure medication, if you lower people's blood pressure too far, they may have sycopy and hit their head and they end up dying of a head injury. So they still died, but at least they have better blood pressure. And so when we talk about the quadricep, when you lock somebody into a quote unquote open kinetic chain, and I keep using quote unquote because I actually don't believe that open and closed kinetic chains are real constructs. Let's just say, ask a mechanical engineer what an open kinetic chain is and their brain will seize on you because it doesn't make any sense to them. But when you think about what you're doing is you're asking, you're putting the quadricep in a situation, say, okay, in a perfect world where nothing can help this, in a total isolation, what can this quadricep do? Now if it lacks that, then it can't be effective. So if it lacks efficacy, so again, your blood pressure medication does nothing for blood pressure, it cannot be effective in the real world. And so the first thing you have to establish is the quadricep efficacious. It knows whether it's going to hurt or not because it's been through that. And so that's really, really kind of super important there. That's where you would train it and start focusing there. No, and from the bottom mechanics perspective, a squat is dealing with a perfectly vertical ground reaction force. When you are decelerating from a run, you are dealing with a horizontal ground reaction force. It has nothing to do with each other as far as that's concerned. What does a leg extension deal with? A horizontal vector. Okay. It is a vector that is perpendicular to the tibia, not axially loading it. Okay. And so that's where when you're decelerating, you are dealing with a perpendicular vector. This is what the 2x2 table would look like. Now here's the problem with sensitivity and specificity. These numbers look pretty good, right? 99.7% specific, 99.7% sensitivity, 0.926% specificity. That looks pretty good. The issue is sensitivity and specificity, number one, they're dichotomous. Spin and snout, we think, okay, rule in, rule out when we have a positive or a negative test. But we know that these tests are not perfect. So even with this test here, that looks pretty good, but this is not one, this is not one. So we know that we're going to have some false positives and that we're going to have some false negatives. So not everybody who tests positive on this test, the ELISA test, actually has the disease. Not everyone that tests negative on this ELISA test is clean. So we can't dichotomize these things. It's more about probability. So that's number one. And number two is sensitivity and specificity are happening in the opposite order that we have it in clinic. This sensitivity is the probability of a positive test result given that we know they have HIV. But in the clinic, we don't know they have HIV. That's why we're testing them, or we don't know that they have the condition. That's why we're testing them. We know that they have or don't have the condition here because we're comparing it to a gold standard. We already have that information. So sensitivity and specificity are just a step to start to appraise the diagnostic utility of a test. It doesn't stop there when we're trying to find the diagnostic utility of a test in clinic. What we actually want to know is the opposite of this. We want to know the probability of the disease given the test result. So we're in clinic. We do a test. We get a positive or a negative. We want to know then, based on this positive or negative result, what's the probability of disease? So we actually want to know the opposite of what sensitivity and specificity are telling us from that regard. And we do that with predictive values. So predictive values are just that. They are the probability of the condition given the test result, which is what happens clinically. So a positive predictive value is the probability of having the disease given a positive test. A negative predictive value is the probability of not having the disease given a negative test. So you test negative in the clinic. You want to know then what's the actual chance that I'm clean. You test positive in the clinic. You want to know, okay, I tested positive, but what's the probability that I actually have the disease then? That's what we want to know. And that's what predictive values give us. And predictive values are given or calculated here. So if you have the sensitivity and the specificity and you have the prevalence data, you can calculate these numbers. And for us, if we have a chart like this, we can actually calculate these numbers really quickly if we have a box like this with all these numbers. Because a positive predictive value is simply this number over, which is our true positives, over the total positives for that test. So 2712 divided by 3251. And that gives us a positive predictive value of .83 for the ELISA test. So that means that if you test positive in the clinic, if you test positive for the ELISA test, there's an 83% chance that you have HIV. So we get sensitivity and specificity by going down the columns and we get predictive values by going across the columns. A negative predictive value, if we use this box over here, can be calculated by our true negative rate, which is this number here, over total negatives of the ELISA test. So 6741 over 6749. And that gives us a negative predictive value of .999. So that means that if you go in the clinic and you take the ELISA test and it's negative, there's a 99.9% chance that you're clean. So that seems pretty good, right? So basically what happens is we have a prior probability of the condition or the prevalence before we test. And if we know that, that's awesome. Whoops. Likely-hood ratios aren't supposed to come up. That's a secret. So our prior probability in this case is our prevalence. .272. That's our prevalence. That's before testing. But we have our prior probability of the condition, which is our prevalence, and then we turn that into a posterior probability of the condition after testing. The issue is predictive values are totally dependent on prevalence data. You can see it here. The prevalence is in both sides of the equation, the numerator and the denominator for both positive predictive values and negative predictive values. The prevalence dictates, if we took this number away, it would, this is the prevalence by the way, it would totally change the entire structure of this table. So if we don't have prevalence data, we can't calculate predictive values. Number one, number two, the prevalence data can only be generalized to that population. See this is lucky here. We've got data on an entire country. But a lot of times you're going to see predictive values in a study that are only for that sample and the prevalence of that sample. That cannot be generalized to the population unless it is a representative sample of the population, unless the prevalence is the same. And a lot of times it is not. So you have to be very careful with trying to generalize predictive values beyond the study, even if you have prevalence data within the study. It's not the exact same as the general population. You can't generalize it. So you just have to be aware of that. So then that's where likelihood ratios come in. Because likelihood ratios are independent of disease prevalence. Likelihood ratios use sensitivity and specificity. They're essentially ratios of two probabilities. So all we need is sensitivity and specificity and we can calculate a likelihood ratio. We don't need the initial prevalence data, which is good. It gets us another step of the way. We are going to still run into the same roadblock. We'll explain that here in a second. But it's nice in that regard. So essentially a likelihood ratio is the probability of getting a result, could be positive or negative, if the patient truly has the condition, over the probability of getting that same positive or negative result, if the patient truly does not have the condition. So in other words, a likelihood ratio of one would mean essentially that the test didn't change anything. It was meaningless. So a likelihood ratio that gets further and further to the right of one, or greater than one, is stronger evidence for the presence of the condition. And a likelihood ratio that gets further away and less and less than one, or further to the left, or less and less than one, is evidence for the absence of the condition. Those essentially tell us to what extent the probability of having the condition is changed after you get a test result. And you'll see positive likelihood ratios or negative likelihood ratios. And that is just referring to the result of the test. So a positive likelihood ratio doesn't mean that the ratio is a positive number, and a negative likelihood ratio means that the ratio is a negative number. That's not what it means. It's always, any likelihood ratio number is always greater, equal to or greater than zero. So it's never a negative number. So a positive likelihood ratio is referring to a positive test result. And it's calculated this way, with sensitivity and specificity. Or in other words, a positive likelihood ratio is the probability of a positive test in those with the disease over the probability of a positive test in those without the disease. So let's say you had a positive likelihood ratio of 10. That would mean that with a positive test, that person walks in with a certain probability of having the disease, they test positive. If that test is a likelihood ratio of 10, that would mean we now have a 10-fold increase in the odds of having the condition. So if you look over here at the ELISA test, that likelihood ratio is 13.4. So in that case, we have a 13.4-fold increase in the odds of having the condition. A negative likelihood ratio is referring to a negative test result. And that's calculated this way. Which is essentially, or it is, the probability of a negative test in those with the disease over the probability of a negative test in those without the disease. So let's say a negative likelihood ratio of 0.1, for example, would mean that with a negative test, we have a 10-fold decrease in the odds of having the condition. So if any test had a negative likelihood ratio of 0.1, then that would significantly decrease the odds of somebody having the disease if they tested negative. And we see over here, the negative likelihood ratio of the ELISA test is 0.003. So that's much less than 0.1. So same idea here. So with the ELISA test, this is a pretty solid test. We don't have anything like this in rehab land. Something that gives you a super strong negative predictive value, pretty strong positive predictive value, which we can increase with two positive tests, which I'll show you in another example. Likelihood ratios of 13 and negative likelihood ratios of 0.003, we just don't have that in rehab land. But this is giving you a nice illustration. Because what if you're already walking in with a very, very, very, very, very, very, very low likelihood of having the condition? Well then in that case, this would just be 13-fold of something that's really, really, really, really, really small that would end up still being small.
